[PATCH net-next v6 7/8] selftests/vsock: test the guest vsock
Author: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>

Add tests for guest vsock device namespace assignment, including ns
destruction and the device moving between namespaces.

The tests drive the device through ynl's cli.py and create guest
namespaces with unshare, so they need python3, PyYAML and unshare. A
missing one skips only these tests, not the whole script.

Signed-off-by: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>

  ioctl helper and its patch

[PATCH net-next v6 5/8] vsock: add a netlink command to assign the
Author: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>

Namespaces let a host isolate a VM's vsock traffic to a specific
namespace, but in a guest vsock traffic cannot be isolated to a
namespace. The vsock device is hardcoded to global mode and can't be
moved into a local-mode namespace.

Introduce a vsock generic netlink family with VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET,
which moves the device to the namespace given by the netns-fd or
netns-pid attribute, as rtnetlink and devlink do, or to the namespace
the request was sent from if neither is given.

VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_GET reads the assignment back, reporting the
namespace as an nsid relative to the caller. VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET
only returns the netlink ack.

The command requires CAP_NET_ADMIN in the initial user namespace. A
privileged user wishing to "unassign" the device can move it to the
init_net, which is hardcoded to global mode (so no unassign call is
necessary).

Connected sockets are closed with ECONNRESET when the underlying device
moves to a different namespace, so as to prevent reachability from the
previous and now disallowed namespace.

Device netns assignment is rejected when it would cause a CID collision
(e.g., moving a g2h device with CID 10 into a namespace with a nested
H2G VM with CID 10). On namespace destruction, the netdev approach
cannot be emulated. A netdev's IP addresses are removed before it
returns to init_net, which makes an address collision impossible, but
the vsock device keeps its CID. Instead, namespace destruction is
deferred until the device has moved. Again, CAP_NET_ADMIN in the init
user namespace is required for the assignment.

Suggested-by: Stefano Garzarella <sgarzare@redhat.com>
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/all/20200427142518.uwssa6dtasrp3bfc@steredhat/
Signed-off-by: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>
---
Changes in v6:
- Move vsock_connect_close() to its own patch (Michal)
- Set swept sockets to TCP_CLOSING instead of TCP_CLOSE, as a peer RST
  does: they were established, and vsock_connect() handles TCP_CLOSING
  as such rather than as a failed connect (Sashiko)
- Take the target namespace from optional netns-fd and netns-pid
  attributes of VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET, falling back to the sender's
  namespace, so userspace no longer has to setns() into the target
  (Stephen)

Changes in v5:
- Rename the uapi header to linux/vsock_netlink.h (Stefano)
- Drop the VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET reply and rely on the netlink ack
  (Stefano)
- Keep vsock_register_mutex static, export vsock_cid_lock() and
  vsock_cid_unlock() for vhost_vsock_set_cid() instead (Stefano)
- Replace vsock_g2h_next_unreachable() with a generic
  vsock_next_connected_socket() next to vsock_for_each_connected_socket(),
  which cannot be reused because the sweep takes lock_sock() (Stefano)
- Use rcu_dereference_protected() in vsock_nl_dev_netns_fill(), drop the
  RCU read lock and allocate the nsid with GFP_KERNEL (Stefano)
- Point to the vsock_g2h_assign_mutex comment for the lock ordering in
  vsock_g2h_net_assign() (Stefano)
- Reverse xmas tree in vsock_dev_do_ioctl() (Stefano)
- Set SOCK_DONE and peer_shutdown on swept sockets, so recv() returns EOF
  and send() fails with -EPIPE/-ECONNRESET instead of -ENOTCONN (Sashiko)
- Only move sock->state back to SS_UNCONNECTED in vsock_connect() if it is
  still SS_CONNECTING, via the new vsock_connect_close(), so a socket
  swept while connect() sleeps cannot be connect()ed again and have
  vsk->trans freed under in-flight skbs (Michal)

Changes in v4:
- Treat an unreachable g2h device as absent in vsock_use_local_transport(),
  so a local ns can connect to the CID it reports as local (Sashiko)
- Reset swept sockets under lock_sock() (Sashiko), and serialize assigns
  with vsock_g2h_assign_mutex now that the sweep runs outside
  vsock_register_mutex

Changes in v3:
- Try to use net_tracker when taking net references
- Take a reference on the net when assigning a device to it. This keeps
  the net alive even after deletion. Drop the concept of moving the
  device back to init_net on net destruction. This avoids the problem
  of the device CID colliding with a nested h2g CID.
- Refuse the assign with -EADDRINUSE when a guest in the target netns
  already answers to the device's CID, via the h2g transport's
  ->has_remote_cid()
- Hold vsock_register_mutex across the CID check and commit in
  vhost_vsock_set_cid(), so it cannot race an assign. We also add a
  lockdep assertion into vsock_find_cid() because it is a requirement to
  avoid racing between netns assignment of a g2h device and setting a
  nested h2g CID (I know, not ideal looking)
- Unlink swept sockets from vsock_connected_table, so a retried
  connect() cannot insert the same node twice
- Drop the RST to the peer from the sweep, along with the ->reset op and
  the pending_reset list (Sashiko)
- Reply to VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET with the netns-id instead a plain ACK
  (Sashiko)

Changes in v2:
- New patch, replaces the ioctl with a genl family and
  VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET (Stefano)
- Make netns_assign_allow a bool, not a callback (Stefano)
- One vsock_netns_assignable(t) helper for both call sites (Stefano)
- Make vsock_g2h_reachable_sk() static, drop the export (Stefano)
- RST the peer when socket loses access to the device's net (Stefano)
- Move the netns reset out of the sysctl exit hook, into the renamed
  vsock_pernet_ops (Stefano).
- Remove the synchronize_rcu() from the pernet exit path. The
  net_namespace documentation states that synchronize_rcu() should be
  avoided in the pernet exit path.
- Run the socket reset in the pernet pre_exit() hook, as the
  net_namespace management code (and documentation) guarantees
  synchronize_rcu() between pre_exit() and exit()
- Drop the conditional synchronize_rcu() in vsock_core_unregister()
  (Stefano)
- Specifically state "G2H transport" in the netns_assign_allow comment (Stefano)
- Avoid leaking the g2h CID to a namespace that cannot reach it;
  GET_LOCAL_CID, bind() and loopback transport selection all read it
  through vsock_registered_transport_cid(), which now takes a netns
- Add VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_GET

[PATCH net-next v6 4/8] vsock: pass the netns to
Author: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>

The transports only use the socket to find its network namespace. Pass
the namespace instead, so that a later patch can ask whether a CID is
taken in a namespace without necessarily having a socket on hand. This
will help with avoiding CID collisions when devices move between
namespaces.

No functional change.

[PATCH net-next v6 0/8] vsock: assign the guest vsock device to a
Author: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>

vsock network namespaces let a host put each VM in a namespace of its
own. A guest has no equivalent yet. It has a single G2H device that
cannot be assigned to a network namespace.

This series lets a guest move that device into a network namespace. A
new generic netlink family, "vsock", supports the command
VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET which assigns the device to the namespace given
by fd or PID, or else to the namespace the request was sent from. The namespace's existing ns_mode then decides who
may use it: a "global" namespace shares the device with every other
global namespace, and a "local" namespace keeps the host connection to
itself. The device starts out in the initial namespace, so until the
command is issued nothing has moved and no mode has changed. There is no
explicit unassign as assigning the device back to the initial namespace
is equivalent.

The command requires CAP_NET_ADMIN in the initial user namespace, so
that an unprivileged user namespace cannot claim the device.

Connections that can no longer reach the device after a move are reset,
so that a namespace which has lost access cannot keep using a socket it
opened while it still had access. The assignment holds a reference on
the namespace, so deleting that namespace does not move the device; only
another assignment does.

Transports opt in through a new netns_assign_allow flag. Only
virtio-vsock sets it here, because it is the only guest transport
that I am able to test against.

Based off of Stefano's original series:
https://lore.kernel.org/all/20200116172428.311437-1-sgarzare@redhat.com/

Suggested-by: Stefano Garzarella <sgarzare@redhat.com>
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/all/20200427142518.uwssa6dtasrp3bfc@steredhat/
Signed-off-by: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>
---

  does: they were established, and vsock_connect() handles TCP_CLOSING
  as such rather than as a failed connect (Sashiko)
- Accept netns-fd and netns-pid attributes in VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET to
  name the target namespace, and test the netns-pid one (Stephen)
- Check reachability in virtio_transport_send_pkt_info() instead of
  virtio_transport_send_pkt(), so RSTs from
  virtio_transport_reset_no_sock() reach the host after an assign

[PATCH net-next v6 1/8] vsock: only undo SS_CONNECTING when
Author: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>

vsock_connect() sleeps with the socket unlocked. Meanwhile the transport
can complete the handshake, setting sock->state to SS_CONNECTED, and then
reset the connection, e.g. in vhost_vsock_reset_orphans(),
virtio_vsock_reset_sock() or vmci_transport_handle_detach(). When
connect() gets the lock back, it finds sk_err set and moves the socket
back to SS_UNCONNECTED. Userspace can then connect() the socket again,
and a connect() to a CID on another transport makes
vsock_assign_transport() release the transport the socket was connected
on.

Only move the socket back to SS_UNCONNECTED if it is still
SS_CONNECTING. A socket that got connected stays SS_CONNECTED, and
another connect() fails with -EISCONN.

Fixes: d021c344051a ("VSOCK: Introduce VM Sockets")
Suggested-by: Michal Luczaj <mhal@rbox.co>
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/netdev/179447093204.2199510.8895677421994612994@kernel.org/
Signed-off-by: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>
---
 net/vmw_vsock/af_vsock.c | 19 +++++++++++++------
 1 file changed, 13 insertions(+), 6 deletions(-)

[PATCH net-next v6 6/8] vsock/virtio: support guest device network
Author: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>

virtio-vsock did not have namespace support (the device was always
accessible to any global namespace).

Make the virtio-vsock device assignable to a namespace by setting
.netns_assign_allow, and allow stream and seqpacket sockets from any
netns that can reach the device rather than only from global-mode ones.
The device starts out assigned to init_net, and because virtio-vsock and
init_net are both hardcoded to global mode, nothing changes until the
assign command is issued.

An assign can land while virtio_transport_rx_work() is draining a
batch, so the assigned namespace is re-read per packet rather than once
per batch. Matching the rest of the batch against the namespace the
device has left would reset connections that belong to the new one.

When the device is assigned, VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET closes every
connected socket that can no longer reach it. A socket that completes
its handshake at the same time must not slip into the connected table
behind that sweep, so the listen and connecting receive paths now call
vsock_maybe_set_connected(), which checks reachability and inserts the
socket under the lock the sweep walks the table with. If the check
fails, the handshake is aborted and the host is sent a RST.

A socket closed by an assign can still try to send, e.g. a credit
update from setsockopt(SO_RCVLOWAT) or a shutdown. To stop that,
virtio_transport_send_pkt_info() now returns -ENETUNREACH without
sending when the socket's netns cannot reach the device. RSTs sent by
virtio_transport_reset_no_sock(), such as the one above, do not go
through it and still reach the host.

Signed-off-by: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>
---
Changes in v6:
- Move the reachability check from virtio_transport_send_pkt() to
  virtio_transport_send_pkt_info(), so RSTs sent by
  virtio_transport_reset_no_sock() still reach the host after an assign
  (Paolo, Sashiko)
- Revert the v5 change that sent the recv_listen()/recv_connecting()
  RSTs to the netns the packet arrived on; sock_net(sk) works again now
  that those RSTs skip the check
- Export vsock_g2h_reachable_sk() for virtio_transport_send_pkt_info()
- Rewrite the commit message: drop what the previous patch already does
  and describe the virtio_transport_send_pkt_info() check (Paolo)

Changes in v5:
- Send the RSTs in virtio_transport_recv_listen() and
  virtio_transport_recv_connecting() to the netns the packet arrived on,
  not the socket's: after an assign the socket's netns may no longer
  reach the device, so the RST would be dropped by the reachability
  check this patch adds to virtio_transport_send_pkt() (Stefano, Sashiko)

Changes in v4:
- Drop packets in virtio_transport_send_pkt() from a netns that cannot
  reach the device, e.g. shutdown() or credit updates on a swept socket
  (Sashiko)

Changes in v3:
- Re-read the assigned netns per packet in virtio_transport_rx_work()
  rather than once per batch, so a mid-batch assign cannot reset the new
  namespace's connections (Sashiko)
- Drop the NULL netns reset in virtio_transport_rx_work(), the assigned
  netns is now pinned
- Drop the .reset op and the virtio_transport_reset{,_no_sock}() exports,
  the sweep no longer sends a RST (Sashiko)

Changes in v2:
- Export virtio_transport_reset(), wire it to the new .reset op (Stefano)
- netns_assign_allow is now a bool (Stefano)
- Pass NULL, not &init_net, in virtio_transport_rx_work(), and
  comment why (Stefano)
- Drop the if (net) guard around put_net() (Stefano)
- Drop the comments at the vsock_maybe_set_connected() call sites,
  the commit msg seems sufficient

[PATCH net-next v6 8/8] selftests/vsock: test the netns assign
Author: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>

VSOCK_CMD_DEV_NETNS_SET refuses callers without CAP_NET_ADMIN in the
init user namespace.

Add two tests: one confirms that CAP_NET_ADMIN is required even by a
privileged user and the other confirms that CAP_NET_ADMIN in an
unprivileged user ns alone is insufficient.

CONFIG_USER_NS is needed to test the CAP_NET_ADMIN + unprivileged user
ns case.

Signed-off-by: Bobby Eshleman <bobbyeshleman@meta.com>
---

  test (Sashiko)

Changes in v4:
- Require EPERM in the privilege tests, cli.py does print the errno, and
  probe unshare -Urn so a setup failure skips (Sashiko)

Changes in v3:

[RFC PATCH] vfio/pci: Wait for GB202 firmware initialization after reset
Author: Gustavo A. <gustavogyn@gmail.com>


On the affected system, GPU passthrough worked with the CachyOS 7.2.10
host kernel. After upgrading the host to 7.3, the guest still booted but
its physical display stayed grey. The exact triggering change is unknown.

GB202 GPUs can complete a PCI reset before their Firmware Security
Processor (FSP) finishes initialization. Wait for the BAR firewall to open,
then poll FSP's boot-complete status before reporting reset success. This
restores normal display output with huge BAR1 mappings.

Use a shared five-second timeout. Reject unassigned BAR0 resources,
temporarily enable memory decoding to read FSP status, and restore the
original decoding state while preserving concurrent command-register
updates.

Apply the check to physical GB202 GPU functions, covering both GeForce
and RTX PRO variants.

Link: https://github.com/NVIDIA/open-gpu-kernel-modules/blob/e6a2e01579fbbd1ca2ed43fef2ee2589e9bd8bd9/src/nvidia/src/kernel/gpu/fsp/arch/blackwell/kern_fsp_gb202.c
Link: https://github.com/NVIDIA/open-gpu-kernel-modules/blob/e6a2e01579fbbd1ca2ed43fef2ee2589e9bd8bd9/src/nvidia/src/kernel/gpu/bif/arch/blackwell/kernel_bif_gb202.c
Signed-off-by: Gustavo A. <gustavogyn@gmail.com>

[RFC PATCH v2 15/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Divert LLDT and LTR into register intercepts
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


HvRegisterCrInterceptControl has LDTR and TR write intercept bits next
to the GDTR and IDTR ones, but only LGDT and LIDT are diverted into
KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_REG_INTERCEPT. An armed LLDT or LTR in the lower VTL
is applied, and the higher VTL never sees the write it asked to
intercept.

Add a divert_seg_write(ctxt, selector, seg) emulator op and call it
from em_lldt() and em_ltr() before load_segment_descriptor(). It goes
through hv_divert_dt_write() with HV_X64_REGISTER_LDTR_ID or
HV_X64_REGISTER_TR_ID; the intercept message carries the written
selector, so the exit reports it in 'base' with a zero 'limit', as
api.rst now says.

Divert from the instruction handlers, before any architectural effect,
rather than from emulator_set_segment(). Task-switch emulation also
loads LDTR through __load_segment_descriptor(), and the TLFS intercepts
cover the LLDT and LTR instructions only; a divert there would drop
the LDTR load of a protected-mode task switch (reachable by a malicious
lower VTL, even though 64-bit Windows takes no task switches) and send
the higher VTL a mid-task-switch intercept with the wrong RIP and
instruction length. For LTR the order also matters because
__load_segment_descriptor() marks the TSS descriptor busy in the GDT,
and a busy descriptor would #GP the LTR when it re-executes after a
cooperative VTL return.

No vmx change is needed: SECONDARY_EXEC_DESC already exits on LTR
(handle_desc covers EXIT_REASON_LDTR_TR) whenever any
reg_intercept_control bit is set.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 26/31] KVM: selftests: Check that a VTL switch keeps bit 63 of RAX
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


An in-kernel VTL call or VTL return hands the entered plane a full
64-bit RAX: the caller's RAX on a call, and on a non-fast return the
lower VTL's RAX from the returning plane's control area. Bit 63 of
that value is the guest's, and must arrive intact whatever the VMM has
enabled.

Make plane 1 set up its VP assist page and return to plane 0, then
have plane 0 make a VTL call with bit 63 of RAX set, and plane 1
answer with a non-fast return whose control area carries a RAX and an
RCX with bit 63 set. Check that each plane sees exactly the value it
was given, with and without KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_RAISE_UD.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 30/31] KVM: Document the two scopes of KVM_REQUEST_PER_PLANE
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A KVM_REQUEST_PER_PLANE request raised through kvm_make_vcpu_request()
(kicks, mass requests) fans out to every plane twin of the vCPU, while
a direct kvm_make_request() marks only the twin it was given. The
per-plane Hyper-V requests use both, and a future direct caller would
trip over the difference. Say so at the definition.

No functional change.

[RFC PATCH v2 06/31] KVM: x86: hyper-v: Handle VTL call/return in the kernel
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


Every VTL transition exits to userspace, which performs the switch
with a dozen cross-thread vCPU ioctls. A Windows guest makes hundreds
of thousands of transitions while booting VSM, so boot spends
*minutes* of wall-clock time in switch overhead alone.

Add two KVM_CAP_HYPERV_FORWARD_HCALL bits for HvCallVtlCall and
HvCallVtlReturn:

 - KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_CALLS (bit 2) gives the two calls their TLFS
   semantics, for a VMM that switches planes itself. Without it they
   stay ordinary hypercalls that KVM does not implement.
 - KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_SWITCH (bit 3), which requires VTL_CALLS,
   handles them in kvm_hv_hypercall().

hyperv_forward_hcall checks that VTL_SWITCH is refused without
VTL_CALLS.

The in-kernel switch copies the VTL-shared state (GPRs except RSP,
CR2) directly between the plane vCPUs, maintains the entered VTL's
assist-page control area (entry reason, caller's RAX/RCX), and
restores RAX/RCX on a non-fast VTL return. The suspended plane keeps
its hypercall completion pending with RIP still at the vmcall, exactly
as userspace-driven switching leaves it. The switch that re-enters a
plane writes the resume value (a full 64-bit register; on a non-fast
return, the lower VTL's own RAX) straight into its RAX, since no
userspace exit is involved, and marks the pending completion to only
retire the vmcall. A target whose vmcall was already retired (left by
a forwarded switch) has no completion armed and simply resumes.

The kernel only switches when it can do so exactly; anything else is
forwarded to userspace, which owns the switch:

 - a target that userspace holds (KVM_PLANE_RUN_STATE_USER_HOLD);
 - a target parked on a completion other than the hypercall one (MMIO,
   PIO), which owns the shared run union;
 - a target plane that has never run, whose HvCallEnableVpVtl state
   only userspace knows. A plane counts as having run once it entered
   the guest on this host or while an in-kernel switch keeps it
   SUSPENDED: last_vmentry_cpu is per-host, and restoring the run
   state is what tells a migration destination that the plane holds
   a context (api.rst says so for KVM_PLANE_RUN_STATE_SUSPENDED);
 - a target whose fpstate layout differs from the caller's;
 - a non-fast return from a plane without a VP assist page whose entry
   registers were not captured by an in-kernel call (userspace entered
   it, or the capture was lost across migration);
 - a VTL call whose control-area write fails. The write goes through
   the entered plane's vCPU, so it passes that plane's memory
   attributes and memslot view, and it happens before any state moves.
   It covers only the fields KVM maintains, the entry reason and
   RAX/RCX: KVM does not emulate VINA, so vina_asserted is the VMM's.

A plane the kernel suspends this way is not a userspace hold. Mark it
KVM_PLANE_RUN_STATE_SUSPENDED at the switch, and clear the flag when
the opposite switch resumes it: the flag gates plane-0 events like the
hold does, and a saved and restored run state puts the suspension back
as it was. The hold stays userspace's alone; in-kernel transitions read
it but never change it.

The SEV-SNP VMPL switch refuses to enter a held plane, failing KVM_RUN
as it does for a missing target, since there is no exit to forward a
VMGEXIT through. It sets neither flag on the VMPL it leaves.

The FPU is shared between VTLs in the TLFS, so the run loop carries it
from the plane that switched away to the plane it entered: the
source's complete fpstate image (XSAVE header included) and
vcpu->arch.pkru are copied to the target. The copy happens as soon as
the source plane's inner run loop has returned, its FPU saved, and
before KVM_RUN can return to userspace. The carry therefore never
outlives the KVM_RUN that performed the switch: after a -EINTR (or any
other) return, KVM_GET_XSAVE and KVM_GET_FPU on the entered plane show
the switched state, and KVM_SET_XSAVE or KVM_SET_FPU need not cancel
anything. Twins share XCR0, and KVM_SET_CPUID enlarges every twin's
fpstate for dynamic xfeatures, as does the creation of a twin after
it, so their XSAVES-compacted layouts match and the copy is exact.
Should they still differ, the switch is forwarded (above) rather than
carried in part; a guest cannot make the host WARN.

A transition the kernel does not handle goes to userspace with the
vmcall already retired and the completion dropped. A completion left
armed across the suspension would fire on whatever entry next resumes
the plane, and after a forced higher-VTL entry that is an arbitrary
interrupted instruction, which must not have RAX overwritten and RIP
skipped. Retiring first also makes the suspended RIP that a higher VTL
reads through GetVpRegisters point past the vmcall.

Retiring at exit time has one wrinkle. With guest EFLAGS.TF and
KVM_GUESTDBG_SINGLESTEP, the skip prepares a KVM_EXIT_DEBUG in the
same run union, on top of the KVM_EXIT_HYPERV just prepared; the
forwarded switch is silently dropped and a debugger-attached Windows
guest wedges. Prepare the hypercall exit again in that case: the VMM
*must* see the switch, at the price of one missed step event. A skip
that failed (SVM without NRIPS emulates it, and a failure prepares
KVM_EXIT_INTERNAL_ERROR) keeps its own exit.

The kvm.hv_vtl_switch_limit module parameter caps how many transitions
the kernel handles, forwarding the rest. It is the bisection knob for
divergence between the two switch paths; only performed switches
count against it.

A suspended plane's completion runs on its next KVM_RUN, after the
higher VTL may have moved it. On a resume from hibernation the secure
kernel sets VTL0's RIP to the hibernate continuation through
HvCallSetVpRegisters, and the deferred completion then skipped an
instruction on top of that RIP. SVM wrote the stale next_rip of the
old VMCALL exit, so the VP resumed at the hypercall page's RET with
the new RSP, popped a zero and faulted at RIP 0: Windows Server 2025
with Credential Guard bugchecked 0xD1 (0, 0xff, 0, 0) on every resume
on AMD (m6a.metal). VMX added the VMCALL length to the new RIP and
landed three bytes into PopSaveHiberContextWrapper+0x35, where the
bytes happen to decode as a harmless instruction, so Intel passed by
luck. The same holds for a FORWARD_HCALL exit completed after
userspace applied such a SetVpRegisters. Record the linear RIP of the
hypercall in cui_linear_rip, as the fast PIO completions do, whenever
a completion is armed, and complete without the skip when the vCPU is
no longer there. A moved vCPU gets the result even when userspace set
KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_UD, rather than a #UD aimed at an instruction it has
left.

The completion armed by an in-kernel switch is marked
hcall_resume_pending until the opposite switch provides the resume
value. A plane resumed any other way (a run-state ioctl, a switch
forwarded to userspace) re-executes the hypercall instead of reading
the run union, which then holds leftover bytes from an unrelated
exit. api.rst describes both guards.

Also report the originating plane of every forwarded hypercall, SynIC
and SynDbg exit, in a field of struct kvm_hyperv_exit that used to be
padding, so userspace can tell which VTL an exit concerns without
tracking every switch.

Co-developed-by: Mushahid Hussain <hmushi@amazon.co.uk>
Signed-off-by: Mushahid Hussain <hmushi@amazon.co.uk>
Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---

[RFC PATCH v2 25/31] KVM: selftests: Check that a higher plane's NMI survives HLT and suspension
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


An NMI sent through the APIC to a non-zero plane is folded back to
plane 0 and leaves only a marker on the targeted plane. When that plane
is the selected one and halted, nothing but the marker can wake it, and
an NMI must break HLT.

Run the plane-1 twin as the selected plane with plane 0 held, halt
it with IF clear, and send an NMI MSI through the plane-1 fd from a
second thread. The twin must leave HLT and reach its next sync point;
if it does not, the thread interrupts KVM_RUN with a signal so the test
fails instead of hanging.

Software-enable the twin's APIC with KVM_SET_LAPIC first. Its plane's
APIC map is built from APIC state writes, and the creation-time reset
runs before the twin joins the plane, so without one the MSI reaches no
vCPU. Assert that KVM_SIGNAL_MSI reports exactly one delivery, so a
lost MSI fails at the send instead of looking like a missed wakeup.

A second case covers an NMI posted to the plane itself. The twin runs
with NMIs blocked (nmi.masked set through KVM_SET_VCPU_EVENTS, as in an
NMI handler before IRET), gets a pending NMI through the same ioctl
(KVM_NMI is not accepted on a plane fd), runs on so the marker is
consumed with the NMI still pending, and is held as a userspace VTL
switch would. Running plane 0 must then report a
KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_VTL_INTERRUPT exit for plane 1. That case needs
KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_CALLS and is skipped without it.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---

[RFC PATCH v2 24/31] KVM: selftests: Check that a restored higher-plane interrupt forces an exit
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A migration destination restores a higher plane's IRR with
KVM_SET_LAPIC, which does not go through __apic_accept_irq() and so
sets no forced-entry marker, and its plane vCPUs have never entered the
guest on that host. KVM must re-derive the marker from the restored
IRR, or an interrupt posted to a suspended VTL1 just before the save is
never delivered.

Model the destination: give a plane-1 twin that has never run an IRR
bit through KVM_SET_LAPIC, leave it held, and require plane 0 to
exit with KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_VTL_INTERRUPT for plane 1. Run it with the
LAPIC restored before and after KVM_SET_PLANE_RUN_STATE.

[RFC PATCH v2 19/31] KVM: x86: Fold an NMI for a higher plane back to plane 0
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


An NMI aimed at a higher plane is injected into that plane, so the
secure kernel takes a real NMI inside VTL1 and bugchecks (0x18B).

For a non-zero plane, inject the NMI into the plane-0 twin instead, to
be delivered once the VTL returns. Set the higher plane's
hv_apic_event_pending marker so the NMI still forces one entry into
it, and kick through __kvm_kick_lower_twins(), the ungated half of
kvm_kick_lower_twins(), since plane 0 takes the NMI whether or not
anything consumes the higher plane's event.
kvm_higher_plane_with_event() counts a higher plane's pending NMI state
(KVM_REQ_NMI, nmi_queued, nmi_pending) as an event as well: the fold
never leaves NMI state there, but KVM_SET_VCPU_EVENTS on the plane's
own fd can.

When the targeted plane is the selected one and halted, the fold must
still break its HLT, as an NMI would on hardware. Its own event checks
see no NMI and plane 0's events are only examined on guest entry, so
kvm_arch_vcpu_runnable() counts the posting marker the fold leaves on
a non-zero plane, and kvm_check_plane0_events() counts plane 0's
nmi_queued, where the folded NMI waits until it is processed.

Plane 0 is looked up without the locks that serialise twin creation,
so its slot is read once, with READ_ONCE(), to pair with its
publication.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 31/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Do not divert descriptor-table writes in SMM
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


Windows VBS boot fails when OVMF executes in SMM. The
hv_divert_dt_write() helper was diverting firmware descriptor-table
writes into the VTL register-intercept path. The failure trace showed a
reflected #GP delivered in an SMM context with IDT base and limit both
zero, followed by a triple fault and guest reset.

Exclude SMM from this helper so those firmware descriptor-table updates
proceed normally, without changing interception for ordinary VTL0
execution.

[RFC PATCH v2 28/31] KVM: SVM: Keep TPR[3:0] across exits without a guest CR8 write
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


V_TPR holds only TPR[7:4]. While KVM does not intercept CR8 writes,
sync_cr8_to_lapic() pushes V_TPR back into the local APIC after every
VMRUN, whether or not the guest touched CR8. A guest that sets the TPR
through the APIC page or the x2APIC MSR therefore loses TPR[3:0] at the
next exit: it writes 0xff, reads back 0xf0, and interrupts in the
priority sub-class it meant to mask are delivered. VMX keeps all eight
TPR bits in the virtual-APIC page and does not have this problem.

Hardware changes V_TPR only on a guest CR8 write. Record the value
sync_lapic_to_cr8() loads on entry and push V_TPR back into the APIC
only when it differs, which only a guest CR8 write can make it do. A
CR8 write whose value matches the loaded TPR[7:4] is indistinguishable
from no write and now preserves TPR[3:0], where architecturally it
would clear it; the previous behaviour cleared TPR[3:0] on every exit
regardless.

Found while implementing Hyper-V VSM on KVM planes.

Fixes: d7bf8221a303 ("KVM: SVM: sync V_TPR with LAPIC.TPR if CR8 write intercept is disabled")
Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/svm/svm.c | 11 ++++++++++-
 arch/x86/kvm/svm/svm.h | 13 +++++++++++++
 2 files changed, 23 insertions(+), 1 deletion(-)

[RFC PATCH v2 14/31] KVM: VMX: Report GVA and instruction bytes on plane-protection faults
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A plane memory-protection fault is reflected to userspace as
KVM_EXIT_MEMORY_FAULT, and a VSM VMM turns it into the TLFS GPA
intercept message for the higher VTL. That message carries the
guest-linear address and the faulting instruction's length and bytes,
none of which the exit reports.

Extend memory_fault with 'gva', 'insn_len' and 'insn_bytes', and a
KVM_MEMORY_EXIT_FLAG_GVA_VALID flag qualifying 'gva'. On VMX, fill them
in handle_ept_violation() while the exit state is still at hand: the
GVA from GUEST_LINEAR_ADDRESS when the exit qualification says it is
valid, the length from VM_EXIT_INSTRUCTION_LEN (architecturally
undefined for EPT violations, but provided by the hardware), and the
bytes read at the linear RIP unless the access was the instruction
fetch itself. The bytes are best effort and zeroed if the read fails.
The plane's protections can refuse that read as well (a protected code
or page-table page is the normal VSM layout), and a refused read
prepares a memory-fault exit of its own; save the exit being reported
around the read so the read cannot replace it.

The new fields fit in the existing kvm_run union and are documented as
present on kernels that advertise KVM_CAP_PLANE_MEMORY_OVERLAYS.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---

 include/uapi/linux/kvm.h       |  6 ++++

[RFC PATCH v2 20/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Honour in_vtl in synthetic cluster IPIs
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A guest using VSM names the target VTL of HvCallSendSyntheticClusterIpi
and HvCallSendSyntheticClusterIpiEx in the input's in_vtl field (TLFS
hv_input_vtl, the byte after the vector). KVM treats that byte as
reserved: the fast form of HvCallSendSyntheticClusterIpi fails any
input with bits above 31 set with HV_STATUS_INVALID_HYPERCALL_INPUT,
and the other forms ignore it.

Decode in_vtl in both the memory and the fast forms and deliver the IPI
into that plane's twin of each targeted VP, or into the sender's own
plane when use_target_vtl is clear. A VP with no twin in the target
plane is skipped. Reserved bits 7:5 of in_vtl are rejected with
HV_STATUS_INVALID_HYPERCALL_INPUT before anything is delivered.

A target VTL above the caller's is refused with HV_STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED,
also for an empty EX set.
Under the VTL privilege model a VTL may interrupt its own level and
lower ones, but never a more privileged one (TLFS §15.9). The target
plane is therefore always at or below the caller's, and so below
KVM_MAX_PLANES.

struct hv_send_ipi and struct hv_send_ipi_ex are shared with the Linux
guest code, which now clears in_vtl and the remaining reserved bytes
separately.

The target twin is looked up without the locks that serialise twin
creation, so read the slot with READ_ONCE() to pair with its
publication.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

 arch/x86/kvm/hyperv.c       | 69 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++---

[RFC PATCH v2 13/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Suppress SynIC exits from higher planes
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


Userspace's SynIC device models plane 0 only. A KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_SYNIC
exit from a higher plane's vCPU looks like a plane-0 SIMP or SIEFP
move, and the VMM relocates the overlay it keeps for that page to the
new GPA, carrying stale overlay contents along.

Do not report SynIC MSR changes of a non-zero plane to userspace; the
SynIC of a higher plane is handled in the kernel. api.rst says so in
the KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_SYNIC description.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 17/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Deliver higher-plane SynIC messages through overlays
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


SynIC message delivery reads and writes a plane's SIMP page in guest
RAM. For a higher plane that page is a per-plane overlay, and the RAM
underneath belongs to plane 0: delivering a higher VTL's message there
corrupts memory plane 0 owns, and judging deliverability from
plane-0-writable bytes lets a lower VTL plant a non-HVMSG_NONE type
and swallow a no_retry (periodic stimer) message.

Read and write the message page through the plane's overlay when one
is installed. A higher plane without an overlay fails the delivery
with -EFAULT instead of falling through to kvm_vcpu_write_guest_page(),
and the sender retries once the overlay appears.

With that, a higher plane's SynIC no longer needs hiding from the VMM.
KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_SYNIC is reported for non-zero planes again, and it and
KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_SYNDBG carry the plane, so the VMM can keep its plane-0
device state separate.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 01/31] KVM: Implement per-plane memory attributes
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


Memory attributes lived solely on struct kvm and the page role carried
no plane identity, so every plane shared one attribute array and one
set of TDP roots. A VTL1 guest protecting VTL0 pages therefore revoked
its *own* access too: the Windows secure kernel's first
HvCallModifyVtlProtectionMask made its own hypercall page
non-executable, and the next VTL transition died on an unhandled
protection fault.

Give each non-zero plane its own attribute xarray. Plane 0 keeps
kvm->mem_attrs, which also carries partition-wide attributes such as
KVM_MEMORY_ATTRIBUTE_PRIVATE. KVM_SET_MEMORY_ATTRIBUTES is accepted on
plane fds, restricted to the NR/NW/NX protection bits, and the plane
joins kvm_mmu_page_role so each plane builds its own roots with its own
protections applied at fault time.

Guest accesses are checked against the attributes of the plane doing
the access, not plane 0's:

 - the vCPU-context hva lookups and kvm_vcpu_map();
 - the guest page read/write helpers, which gain a kvm_plane argument
   (the VM-level variants keep checking plane 0);
 - the software page walker's PTE reads;
 - the emulator's memory-fault classification (an access its own plane
   protects becomes KVM_EXIT_MEMORY_FAULT, not MMIO) and its instruction
   fetch NX check;
 - the slow-form Hyper-V hypercall inputs (TLB flush, send IPI and the
   sparse VP sets and flush entries they read) and the pvclock wall
   clock written by an MSR, all of which run with the issuing vCPU at
   hand and so switch from kvm_read_guest()/kvm_write_guest() to the
   vCPU variants.

Without that, a higher plane is refused access to pages it has itself
protected against plane 0; the secure kernel's VP assist page
registration and the walks through its own protected page tables both
fail exactly there.

Cached gfn->hva translations (gfn_to_hva_cache and pfncache, used for
steal time, PV EOI and pvclock) have no plane context at use time, so
they are refused when *any* plane protects the range with NR or NW.

Hugepage mixed-attribute tracking still follows plane 0 only. Faults
on other planes clamp to 4KiB whenever the plane carries any attribute
within the maximal mapping range, and TDP MMU huge-page recovery skips
their roots, so a recovered 2MiB or 1GiB SPTE cannot lift a 4KiB
protection on a neighbouring gfn.

The plane arrays are memcg-accounted like plane 0's. An attribute update
that fails part-way now releases the xa_reserve() placeholders it left
behind, on the VM fd as well as on plane fds, so a failed update cannot
look like an attribute to the range lookups.

role.smm gives up its eighth bit to make room for role.plane. It
only had a whole byte to be faster to load, and it stays at the top
of the word so kvm_memslots_for_spte_role() still extracts it with a
shift.

Mushahid Hussain supplied the !KVM_GENERIC_MEMORY_ATTRIBUTES stub of
kvm_plane_get_memory_attributes() that keeps the other architectures
building.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---

[RFC PATCH v2 27/31] KVM: SVM: Carry instruction state on reflected plane-protection faults
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A plane-protection fault reflected to userspace becomes a TLFS GPA
intercept message, which carries the faulting instruction's length,
its bytes (for data accesses), and the fault-adjusted RFLAGS.  The
VMX exit handler harvests all of that from the VMCS, but
npf_interception() reflected the bare fault: on AMD the higher VTL
received intercept messages with instruction length 0, no instruction
bytes and RFLAGS.RF clear, failing the hyperv_vsm KUT
intercept-message tests ("Seen an expected read/write intercept
message").

Decode Assists store up to 15 bytes fetched from CS:RIP in the VMCB on a
data-access #NPF (the raw window, not the decoded length); run them
through the instruction decoder and report the decoded length and that
many bytes. When the window is empty (no Decode Assists, a fault while
fetching CS:RIP, or erratum 1096), decode from guest memory at RIP
instead, except for SEV guests whose memory KVM cannot read. The
decoder's own fetches can be refused by the plane's protections (a
protected code or page-table page is the normal VSM layout) and then
prepare a memory-fault exit of their own, so the exit being reported is
saved around the decode, as around the VMX read. Set RFLAGS.RF before
exiting: fault delivery gives the restarted
instruction RF=1, and VMX saves that fault-adjusted value in guest
RFLAGS on fault-type exits while SVM saves RF as it was during
execution.

An instruction-fetch #NPF stores no bytes (APM vol 2, "Nested and
Intercepted #PF"). The length an execute intercept carries is that of
the branch that fetched the faulting page, which VMX reports and SVM
does not record; decoding at RIP would describe the branch target
instead. Execute intercepts therefore carry length 0 on SVM.

SEV-ES and SEV-SNP guests get the bare fault: KVM cannot see the CS and
mode state the decoder would use, and RFLAGS lives in the encrypted
VMSA, where a VMCB write does nothing.

Both harvests act on KVM_MEMORY_EXIT_FLAG_PLANE_PROT, which marks a
memory-fault exit caused by the plane's memory attributes. It is set
where the attribute check refuses the access, by
kvm_mark_plane_prot_fault(): in the fault-in path, in the guest
page-table walker, in kvm_prepare_memory_fault_exit_for_range() (the
emulator's data accesses) and on the emulator's instruction fetch. So an
emulated access carries it as well as a guest #NPF. Inferring it from
the shape of a walker exit instead would also tag
nested_svm_get_tdp_pdptr()'s exit for an nCR3 outside guest memory, and
set RF on a fault that is not a plane intercept at all. Exits KVM cannot
attribute (an unmapped GPA, the hypercall buffer and kvmclock checks)
leave it clear.

The validity of the harvested state gets its own flag,
KVM_MEMORY_EXIT_FLAG_INSN_VALID, set by the VMX and SVM harvests only
when they populated the fields: VM_EXIT_INSTRUCTION_LEN is undefined
for EPT violations, all-zero insn_bytes is itself a valid x86
encoding, and kvm_prepare_memory_fault_exit() now clears
gva/insn_len/insn_bytes so no other memory-fault exit carries stale
values.  The flag bit (and PLANE_PROT before it) is enumerated by the
plane memory overlay capability, which also covers the extended
memory_fault layout.  The memory_attributes selftest asserted strict
equality on the exit flags; it now masks the informational flags out
and compares only the access that faulted. api.rst documents both
flags.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 09/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Divert intercepted descriptor-table loads
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


The secure kernel arms descriptor-table write intercepts for VTL0 with
HvRegisterCrInterceptControl, and PatchGuard then runs its protected
patching window under an intercepted LIDT. The write must not be
applied: the window would run with a genuinely null IDT and the first
event triple-faults the guest. KVM has no notion of the intercept, so
it loads the IDT.

Add a per-vCPU KVM_CAP_HYPERV_REG_INTERCEPT through which the VMM sets
the control word. While a descriptor-table write bit is armed, the
vendor code traps descriptor-table loads, and the emulator diverts an
armed LGDT or LIDT executed in plane 0 into a
KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_REG_INTERCEPT exit instead of applying it. The exit
carries the HV_X64_REGISTER_* id, the base and limit the guest tried to
load, and the RIP and length of the instruction. RIP is rewound to the
instruction: the higher VTL owns completion, and Windows'
patch-protection probe matches its reflected #GP(0) against the probe
RIP. The diverted write is consumed before x86_emulate_instruction()
returns, so it never outlives the emulation.

api.rst documents the exit and its reg_intercept payload.

VMX traps with SECONDARY_EXEC_DESC; SVM arms the four VMCB
descriptor-table load intercepts as a block to match the all-or-nothing
VMX control, and routes the exits into kvm_emulate_instruction(). A
load the divert check declines is applied by the emulator as before.
Store intercepts stay untrapped, as the control has no bits for them.

The (dis)arming gets its own kvm_x86_ops.update_hv_reg_intercepts(),
touching only the L1 controls, rather than riding on hooks with
lifecycles of their own:

 - vmx_set_cr4() owns SECONDARY_EXEC_DESC for UMIP emulation, so a
   VTL0 set-then-clear of CR4.UMIP would disarm the very lidt/lgdt trap
   this exists for. The CR4 path preserves the control while the
   intercepts are armed.
 - On VMX the op defers while vmcs02 is loaded, and nested_vmx_vmexit()
   refreshes vmcs01 after switching back, rather than writing vmcs02
   state that the next nested VM-exit would throw away.
 - On SVM it arms vmcb01, and nested_vmcb02_recalc_intercepts() strips
   the LOAD_* bits from vmcb02 unless vmcb12 wants them, matching the
   VMX DESC strip. hv_divert_dt_write() bails in guest mode, so an L2
   descriptor exit that still reaches the emulator (UMIP emulation,
   for example) applies the load normally.
 - init_vmcb() re-applies the SVM intercepts, since reg_intercept_control
   survives a vCPU reset: a guest SHUTDOWN clears the VMCB and resets
   the vCPU, and must not leave them disarmed behind the higher VTL's
   back.

Arming is refused with -EINVAL where the diverted load cannot be
emulated, before anything is armed; disarming always succeeds:

 - TDX does not allow descriptor-table loads to be intercepted.
 - On SEV-ES, desc_interception() hits svm_check_emulate_instruction()
   returning X86EMUL_RETRY_INSTR, which resumes at the same RIP with
   the intercept still armed, so one descriptor-table load would spin a
   vCPU (and a host CPU) forever.
 - On plain SEV, the emulator has no instruction bytes and would inject
   #UD on an architecturally valid lgdt.

For the same reason KVM_CAP_VM_MOVE_ENC_CONTEXT_FROM refuses to turn a
VM into an SEV guest while any of its vCPUs has the intercepts armed;
the VMM can disarm first. A failure to re-apply the intercepts after
that is a broken invariant, so svm_vcpu_after_set_cpuid() and
init_vmcb() warn and disarm rather than leave reg_intercept_control
claiming intercepts the VMCB does not have.

The cap stays refused on plane fds, like the other unaudited vCPU caps:
the divert only ever applies to plane 0.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---

[RFC PATCH v2 11/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Make KVM_REQ_HV_STIMER a per-plane request
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A synthetic timer that expires on a suspended higher plane raises
KVM_REQ_HV_STIMER on that plane's vCPU. The request is shared between
the plane twins, so the plane that is running consumes it and processes
its own synthetic timers, not the twin's. The twin's expired timer is
never delivered.

Define KVM_REQ_HV_STIMER with KVM_ARCH_PLANE_REQ(), so each plane keeps
its own request and processes it when it next runs.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 12/31] KVM: x86: Kick lower twins when an event lands on a suspended plane
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


An interrupt or synthetic timer expiry posted to a suspended higher
plane raises a per-plane request on that plane's vCPU and kicks it. The
kick reaches the lower twin running on the same vCPU thread, since the
twins share their mode and wait queue, but the per-plane request does
not: a twin that is already past its event checks enters the guest
anyway, and the event does not preempt it until something else makes it
exit.

Add kvm_kick_lower_twins(), which raises KVM_REQ_UNBLOCK, a request in
the word the twins share, and kicks, so the running twin aborts a
vmentry in flight and re-evaluates pending higher-plane events. Call it
after delivering a fixed interrupt to a non-zero plane and whenever a
synthetic timer is marked pending, including the retry paths that call
stimer_mark_pending() with vcpu_kick false: a timer that expires again
on a parked plane through a retry must preempt the lower plane too. A
LAPIC timer expiry already raises KVM_REQ_UNBLOCK and kicks in
apic_timer_expired(), so that path needs nothing more.

The kick is skipped when the event cannot be consumed: without
KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_CALLS, or, with in-kernel switching, on a plane the
forced entry would refuse. kvm_higher_plane_event_consumable() holds
that rule, and kvm_higher_plane_with_event() now uses it too.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 07/31] KVM: selftests: Cover single-step over a forwarded VTL call
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A forwarded HvCallVtlCall retires the vmcall before the exit is
delivered, and under KVM_GUESTDBG_SINGLESTEP the deferred skip used to
overwrite the KVM_EXIT_HYPERV exit with KVM_EXIT_DEBUG, silently

The stage runs only where KVM reports KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_CALLS;

[RFC PATCH v2 03/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Maintain a reference TSC page for each plane
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


HV_X64_MSR_REFERENCE_TSC is private to each Virtual Trust Level: the
secure kernel maps its own page from VTL1, as the normal kernel does
from VTL0. With one partition-wide MSR, VTL1 enabling its page replaced
VTL0's, and the normal kernel fell back to a TIME_REF_COUNT MSR read (a
VM exit) on every clock read.

The reference time behind the pages is one clock per partition, so
every page shows the same scale, offset and sequence. Keep the MSR per
plane, and have every masterclock update write the shared parameters
to every enabled page. A plane that enables its page while another
page is valid gets the current parameters at once, rather than an
update that invalidates every page for nothing. A host-side MSR
restore leaves the page alone, so a page migrated with the guest keeps
its contents.

The pages are guest-writable, so KVM never trusts what it reads back
from them; the TSC-emulation case below keeps such values away from
higher planes. A page is invalidated with a discrete zero-sequence write
before its body changes: the stores of one long copy are not ordered as
another vCPU sees them. The new sequence must also differ from the one
the page held, or a reader that sampled the old sequence accepts a torn
read across the rewrite. A host-side counter (hv_tsc_sequence) is not
enough for that. It is not migrated, so on a destination it restarts
below the sequence on the restored pages, and the first publish there
can repeat it. And under TSC emulation the pages carry a sequence the
guest wrote: VTL0 could stage a value on a higher plane's page and pump
recomputes, one MSR write at a time, until the counter reaches it. So
each update takes the highest of hv_tsc_sequence and every enabled
page's current sequence, plus one, skipping 0, 0xFFFFFFFF and any value
a page holds. The pages can push the sequence past their values but
never make KVM repeat one, and the choice needs no new migrated state.
When a plane enables its page while the parameters are valid, the
parameters go to that page alone, unless the page holds the current
sequence over a different body: then it takes the full update. A page
that already shows exactly the current contents is left alone.

Each page is written and read as the plane that owns it
(kvm_plane_write_guest(), kvm_plane_read_guest()), under that plane's
own memory attributes. A refused access costs only that plane: its page
is marked in a per-plane broken bitmap and KVM stops maintaining it,
leaving whatever it last held, until the plane writes its MSR again.
When the master clock is not TSC-based, no parameters can be computed
and every enabled page is left invalid, with its readers on the
TIME_REF_COUNT fallback, until a later clock update succeeds.

With HV_X64_MSR_TSC_EMULATION_CONTROL set (reenlightenment, restored
across migration), kvm_hv_setup_tsc_page() keeps the parameters it
reads back from the lowest enabled plane's page, with the reserved
field cleared, and invalidates any other page that does not match them.
That page is writable by the lowest VTL, so its contents must not become
a higher VTL's clock: a higher plane's TIME_REF_COUNT, synthetic timers
and timer message timestamps answer from kvmclock rather than from the
read-back parameters, and an MSR write in this state takes a full
update instead of being handed them. Without the first part, VTL0
could stage parameters on its own page, arm TSC emulation and wait for
any masterclock update (migration, KVM_SET_CLOCK, host suspend): KVM
would invalidate VTL1's page and leave the secure kernel reading a
TIME_REF_COUNT that VTL0 chose.

Co-developed-by: Mushahid Hussain <hmushi@amazon.co.uk>
Not-signed-off-by: Mushahid Hussain <hmushi@amazon.co.uk>
Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---

 arch/x86/include/asm/kvm_host.h |  28 ++-
 arch/x86/kvm/hyperv.c           | 377 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++----

 virt/kvm/kvm_main.c             |  50 +++++

[RFC PATCH v2 22/31] KVM: selftests: Test a plane suspending with an undelivered interrupt
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


Pin the forced-VTL-entry re-arm: a plane-1 twin self-IPIs with IF
clear, runs on (consuming its single-shot hv_apic_event_pending
marker), and is then suspended by KVM_SET_PLANE_RUN_STATE exactly like
a userspace VTL switch.  Running plane 0 must report the suspended
twin's deliverable interrupt as a KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_VTL_INTERRUPT exit,
and the re-entered twin must receive the vector once it sets IF.  On a
kernel without the re-arm, plane 0 reaches its guest code and the
twin's interrupt is stranded in its IRR, which is the Windows VSM
hibernate deadlock in miniature.  The VM enables
KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_CALLS, which VTL interrupt exits require.

The default selftest VM owns a fully in-kernel irqchip, which planes
refuse; add a kvm_force_split_irqchip override to the library (as in
the public planes tree) so a planes test can use the stock VM setup.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 21/31] KVM: x86: Gate and re-arm forced VTL entry on interrupt deliverability
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A fixed vector posted to a higher plane forces a VTL entry even when
the higher VTL's TPR masks it, so the secure kernel is entered with
reason INTERRUPT and nothing to deliver. The TLFS (§15.9, "VTL
Interrupt Management") holds such an interrupt pending without
switching: only a *deliverable* interrupt preempts the lower VTL.

Gate forced entry on the higher plane's APIC reporting a deliverable
vector under its PPR. A masked vector keeps its posted marker instead
of consuming the single entry attempt, so the entry fires if a later
VTL round trip leaves the vector deliverable; the higher plane still
consumes its own marker when it runs. Each marker is consumed only by
its own clause, in both the userspace exit and the in-kernel refusal
paths, so an exit triggered by ExtINT, a stimer or the LAPIC timer
while the posted vector is still masked leaves the APIC marker armed.
The gate and the consumption share kvm_vtl_event_deliverable() and
kvm_vtl_event_consume(), so the two cannot drift apart.

NMIs get a marker of their own, hv_nmi_event_pending, and keep forcing
entry unconditionally; the NMI is folded back to plane 0 on
acceptance, so the higher plane's APIC has nothing the PPR gate could
see.

 - The marker is armed wherever NMI state is posted to a non-zero
   plane: the APIC fold and KVM_SET_VCPU_EVENTS on the plane's fd. No
   other path reaches a higher plane: KVM_NMI is not on the plane-fd
   allowlist, and sync_regs apply to plane 0, whose fd runs KVM_RUN.
 - It is also what breaks the HLT of a halted selected plane that the
   fold targets (kvm_arch_vcpu_runnable()).
 - The level-triggered NMI state (KVM_REQ_NMI, nmi_queued,
   nmi_pending) no longer feeds the gate. A posting earns exactly one
   wakeup, as on the APIC path, rather than holding the gate true for
   a parked higher plane.
 - KVM_SET_VCPU_EVENTS that retracts the plane's own NMI takes back the
   marker it earned. A marker left by an NMI folded to plane 0 stays,
   since that NMI is not the plane's. vCPU reset clears both markers.

The in-kernel switch consumes the markers on a refused entry attempt
too. The only refusal left on that path is a failing VTL-control write
(blocked planes are filtered in kvm_higher_plane_with_event()), which
stays failed until an overlay or memory-attribute change. The event
stays latched in the plane's own pending state for its next entry,
instead of repeating the failing guest write on every exit of the
lower plane and keeping the vCPU from ever blocking.

The hyperv_vsm conformance test for TPR-masked vectors accepts either
behaviour.

The marker is single-shot, and consuming it does not mean the vector
was delivered. Windows hibernation posts vector 0xE0 to a
VTL1 twin mid-run with IF clear: the run consumes the marker without
injecting, the twin suspends with the vector in its IRR, and nothing
posts it again. VTL0 freezes all processors, spins waiting on the
secure kernel, and wedges the VM at 100% host CPU. A migration loses the
marker the same way: KVM_SET_LAPIC restores the IRR without going
through __apic_accept_irq(), so a vector posted to a suspended VTL1
just before the save never forces an entry on the destination.

Add a kvm_arch_vcpu_plane_stopped() hook, called from
kvm_vcpu_set_plane_stopped() for both a VtlReturn and the run-state
ioctl, with an empty weak default. On x86 it re-arms
hv_apic_event_pending when a non-zero plane stops with a vector latched
in its IRR, and KVM_SET_LAPIC on a stopped non-zero plane does the
same, so a destination re-derives the marker whichever of the two it
restores last. A re-armed marker cannot storm: the deliverability gate
on forced entry still applies.

An NMI posted to the plane itself is lost the same way: a run with
NMIs blocked (inside an NMI handler, before IRET) consumes
hv_nmi_event_pending and leaves the NMI latched, and the lower VTL
waiting on the higher one wedges. The hook re-arms hv_nmi_event_pending
too when the plane stops with an NMI injected, pending, queued or
requested. The NMI clause is ungated, so this costs at most one forced
entry each time the plane is stopped.

Nothing is armed for a plane whose event cannot be consumed
(kvm_higher_plane_event_consumable()), which also keeps VMs without
KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_CALLS out of it; that matters because the hook also
runs when __sev_snp_run_vmpl() switches VMPLs.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 02/31] KVM: x86: hyper-v: Bank GUEST_OS_ID and HYPERCALL MSRs per plane
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


The TLFS makes HV_X64_MSR_GUEST_OS_ID and HV_X64_MSR_HYPERCALL
separate instances per VTL; KVM kept a single partition-wide copy, so
the Windows secure kernel enabling its own hypercall page from VTL1
clobbered VTL0's.

Index both MSRs by the vCPU's plane. The hypercall page contents are
still written into guest RAM on enable, now once per plane's page.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 10/31] KVM: x86: Keep APICv off on higher-plane vCPUs
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A higher-plane twin is switched in software and spends most of its
time suspended. With APICv active, an interrupt posted to it sits in
the PIR, where the lower plane's checks for a pending higher-plane
event cannot reliably see it while the twin is not running.

Leave APICv inactive on every vCPU with a non-zero plane_level: at LAPIC
creation, and in __kvm_vcpu_update_apicv() so a later APICv update
cannot turn it back on. Higher planes use the legacy delivery path,
whose IRR the pending-event checks read directly.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/lapic.c | 8 +++++++-
 arch/x86/kvm/x86.c   | 6 +++++-
 2 files changed, 12 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-)

[RFC PATCH v2 16/31] KVM: Add per-plane memory overlays
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


TLFS overlay pages, the SynIC message page (SIMP) in particular, show
each VTL a private page at a GPA while the other VTLs keep seeing the
guest RAM underneath. With planes, all planes share the memslots, so a
VMM has no way to give a single plane a different backing for one gfn.

Add the KVM_SET_PLANE_MEMORY_OVERLAY vm ioctl, which pins one page of
userspace memory and maps it over a gfn for a single plane, replaces
it, or removes it when 'uaddr' is 0. KVM_CAP_PLANE_MEMORY_OVERLAYS
reports the per-plane limit (KVM_PLANE_MAX_OVERLAYS, 64). An overlay
cannot cover a private or guest_memfd-only gfn.

On x86, per-plane EPT roots make a per-plane pfn override in
__kvm_mmu_faultin_pfn() sufficient. While a plane has overlays its
mappings stay at 4KiB, so a neighbouring fault's huge page cannot span
an overlaid gfn, and the gmem path only ever narrows max_level.
kvm_tdp_mmu_recover_huge_pages() skips non-zero-plane roots, and plane
0 roots while plane 0 has overlays, so a later promotion cannot widen
a plane's attribute or overlay clamps back out. Planes keep no lpage
tracking; their fault path rebuilds each mapping at the largest level
the plane allows.

The overlay update runs under the kvm_mmu_invalidate_start()/end()
protocol with the gfn registered, as the mmu notifiers do, so a
concurrent lockless fault cannot install an SPTE for the old backing
after the zap. Overlays are unpinned when the plane is destroyed.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---

[RFC PATCH v2 23/31] KVM: selftests: Check that a masked higher-plane timer is no event
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


A LAPIC timer that expires under a masked LVTT latches the expiry but
is never injected. A higher plane that arms its timer and then masks
LVTT must therefore not count as having a pending event, or every run
of the lower plane ends in a KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_VTL_INTERRUPT exit (or a
forced entry) with nothing for the higher plane to take.

The plane-1 twin arms a 50ms one-shot timer, masks LVTT and is
suspended like a userspace VTL switch. Once KVM_GET_LAPIC shows the
timer expired, plane 0 must run its guest code to GUEST_DONE.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>

[RFC PATCH v2 05/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Don't arm PV EOI for a higher plane's VP assist page
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


Registering the VP assist page arms the PV EOI / APIC assist
optimisation through a gfn_to_hva cache, and that cache refuses ranges
carrying RW memory protections in plane 0's view. The Windows secure
kernel places its VTL1 assist page in memory it has protected against
VTL0, so the MSR write failed and VTL1 had no assist page at all: no
VTL entry reasons, no VTL-return register control, no boot.

Register the page without arming PV EOI when the vCPU is in a plane
above 0. KVM then never sets the no-EOI-required bit for that plane,
so the guest always performs a real EOI; only the fast path is lost.
kvm_hv_assist_page_enabled() reports false for any plane above 0, so
that nothing else (MSR_KVM_PV_EOI_EN on that plane included) can arm
the assist-page consumers there.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/hyperv.c | 30 +++++++++++++++++++++++++-----
 1 file changed, 25 insertions(+), 5 deletions(-)

[RFC PATCH v2 04/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Flush every plane twin on TLB flush hypercalls
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


The Hyper-V TLB flush hypercalls resolve their target vCPUs through
the plane-0 array, so a flush only ever reached the plane-0 vCPUs, and
the caller's own plane and every other plane kept stale translations.
Two Windows VSM failures come from this. The secure kernel repopulated
a PFN-database page table entry, flushed, then took a fatal page fault
on the very address it had just mapped, because VTL1's TLB still held
the old read-only zero-page translation. And an SMP boot bugchecks 0x1A
(MEMORY_MANAGEMENT, 0x6102) minutes in, when VTL0 reads stale PTEs that
the secure kernel has already edited in mappings both VTLs use.

The TLFS lets a flush cover more than the caller asked for, and does
not return from the hypercall until the targets have left guest mode.
Enqueue the flush entries for every twin of each target VP, and raise
KVM_REQ_HV_TLB_FLUSH through the waiting request fan-out, so the
hypercall waits for every plane, whichever VTL issued it. Make the
request per plane: it lives in the request word the twins share, and a
plane-0 twin consuming it with an empty fifo would otherwise drop a
flush queued for another plane.

The walk reaches twins whose mutex the caller does not hold, so it
leaves the Hyper-V context check to hv_tlb_flush_enqueue(), which reads
it with to_hv_vcpu_safe() and skips a twin that has none yet.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---
 arch/x86/include/asm/kvm_host.h |  3 ++-
 arch/x86/kvm/hyperv.c           | 41 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++---
 2 files changed, 40 insertions(+), 4 deletions(-)

[RFC PATCH v2 08/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Let higher-VTL interrupts preempt a lower VTL
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


In the TLFS, an interrupt pending for a higher VTL preempts execution
in a lower VTL (TLFS §15.2.4), and the secure kernel depends on it, for
example to freeze the other processors' VTL1 while it starts a new one.
KVM never looks at a suspended higher plane while a lower plane runs,
so the interrupt waits until the lower VTL calls up, and a Windows VSM
boot stalls.

kvm_higher_plane_with_event() reports the nearest higher plane with a
pending ExtINT, synthetic-timer request or LAPIC timer expiry, or an
interrupt posted to it while suspended. Such an event also makes the
lower vCPU runnable. Nothing is reported without
KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_CALLS, since only the VTL contract consumes it.

Interrupts force entry edge-triggered, one attempt per posting, not
level-triggered on the plane's IRR: a vector that stays latched,
because an entry was refused (no VP assist page yet) or the higher
plane's TPR masks it, would otherwise retry the entry on every run of
the lower plane and be replayed the moment the assist page is enabled.
Delivering a fixed vector to a non-zero plane sets
hv_apic_event_pending on it. The marker is cleared by a
KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_VTL_INTERRUPT exit for the plane, and by a run of the
plane itself that reaches event evaluation; a run aborted by the VTL
exit or KVM_REQ_PLANE_RESCHED bail-outs in vcpu_enter_guest() leaves it
set, since nothing would re-arm it for a TPR-masked vector.

With KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_CALLS alone, userspace switches planes: KVM
exits with the new KVM_EXIT_HYPERV_VTL_INTERRUPT naming the higher
plane, from vcpu_enter_guest() and from the halted path in vcpu_run(),
so the VMM enters that VTL and its view of the active VTL stays in sync.
With KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_VTL_SWITCH, kvm_hv_vtl_interrupt_entry() enters
the higher plane in the kernel, writing the INTERRUPT entry reason and
the preempted RAX/RCX into its control area through the entered plane.
The lower plane resumes at the preempted RIP; a failed write leaves the
event pending.

kvm_hv_vtl_entry_blocked() names the targets the in-kernel paths must
not enter: a plane that never ran, one userspace holds, and one
parked on a completion other than the hypercall one. Forced entry
refuses them, and in that mode kvm_higher_plane_with_event() skips
their events, or a halted lower VTL would spin through hlt/wake on an
event nothing can consume. In userspace mode the exit lets the VMM
consume them, so they still count.

The forced entry follows the VTL call's rules for what it writes and
carries: only the entry reason and RAX/RCX of the control area (the
VMM owns vina_asserted), and the FPU through the same run-loop carry,
which also leaves the event pending when the twins' fpstates differ.
A target parked in a hypercall of its own keeps the copied RAX, and
its pending completion only retires the vmcall.

A timer expiry counts once it has fired but not been injected, since
injection only happens when the plane runs. It is tested with
kvm_cpu_has_pending_timer(), the check that gates the injection:
apic_timer_expired() latches an expiry whatever the LVTT mask says, and
a higher VTL that arms its timer and then masks LVTT would otherwise
leave an event that nothing ever injects, forcing the lower VTL into
the higher one, or out to userspace, on every entry.

A higher plane's LAPIC timer can ride the VMX preemption timer, which
only counts in VMX non-root mode, so a suspended plane's timer would
never fire. When KVM_RUN on a non-zero plane ends with the hv timer in
use, move the timer to the hrtimer, which keeps counting while the
plane is parked, and move it back when the plane next runs.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---

[RFC PATCH v2 18/31] KVM: x86: hyperv: Truncate higher-VTL vAPIC MSR writes
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


The Windows secure kernel writes the Hyper-V vAPIC MSRs, HV_X64_MSR_EOI
among them, with stale data in bits 63:32. Those bits are reserved for
every register but the ICR, so KVM injects #GP, and a #GP there is a
VTL1 bugcheck.

Truncate non-ICR writes through kvm_hv_vapic_msr_write() from a
non-zero plane to 32 bits. The exemption covers only this paravirtual
path; a native x2APIC WRMSR still takes the architectural #GP on any
plane.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/lapic.c | 12 ++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 12 insertions(+)

[RFC PATCH v2 00/31] KVM: x86: Hyper-V VSM (Virtual Trust Levels) on VM planes
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


This is v2 of the Hyper-V VSM support¹: Windows Virtualization Based
Security running natively on KVM planes, with the secure kernel in
VTL1 isolated from the normal kernel in VTL0, instead of using nested
virtualization.

This is still largely AI-written with minimal oversight so far; it's
presented to show the direction and for testing, not for detailed
review.

As before, it boots Windows Server 2016 through 2025 with Credential
Guard active, on both Intel and AMD hosts, with VTL1 protecting its
memory from VTL0, and survives hibernate/resume. It also runs VBS
enclaves: the full CreateEnclave / LoadEnclaveImage / InitializeEnclave
/ CallEnclave cycle behaves as it does on real Hyper-V. The userspace
side is still a separable, permissively-licensed library, pulled into
QEMU for testing.

Since v1:

 • Rebased to v7.4-rc2. Paolo's memory protection attributes are now
   merged in from kvm.git² rather than carried as patches.
 • Three parts grew up and left home: the fixes to Jörg's series³,
   the forwarding of unimplemented Hyper-V hypercalls to userspace⁴
   and the generic plane userspace API⁷ are posted separately, and
   this series is based on them.
 • This is now the *minimum* VSM: what it takes to boot Windows with
   Credential Guard and keep VTL1's memory protected. The parts it
   can live without — KVM_TRANSLATE on plane fds, VTL-switch
   statistics, the Hyper-V vCPU suspended state and the TLB flush
   inhibit (KVM_HYPERV_SET_TLB_FLUSH_INHIBIT) — sit above it in git⁵
   and will follow once this part makes sense to people.
 • KVM_TRANSLATE2 is gone. The userspace library walks the guest
   page tables itself for HvTranslateVirtualAddress, which it turned
   out to need to do anyway.
 • The hypercall exit gained a flags field (KVM_HYPERV_HCALL_UD) for
   fault injection instead of stealing bit 63 of the result, so a
   forwarded hypercall can return a full 64-bit result.
 • A pile of fixes from review, each noted in its commit.

The base is Jörg's KVM Planes + SEV-SNP series⁶ with the fixes³ and
the plane userspace API⁷ applied; kvm-memprot-v4² and the hypercall
forwarding⁴ are merged on top. None of that is reposted here. The
whole assembly, and the userspace to drive it:

 • https://git.infradead.org/?p=users/dwmw2/linux.git;a=shortlog;h=refs/heads/vsm2/vsm-min (tag vsm2-min1)
 • https://git.infradead.org/?p=users/dwmw2/qemu.git;a=shortlog;h=refs/heads/vsm-v2
 • https://git.infradead.org/?p=users/dwmw2/kvm-unit-tests.git;a=shortlog;h=refs/heads/vsm-v2

The 31 patches here are VSM itself: per-plane memory
attributes and overlays, the per-plane Hyper-V state (GUEST_OS_ID,
hypercall page, reference TSC page, SynIC, STIMER), VTL call/return
and the VTL-protection fault path, interrupt preemption and forced
VTL entry, and the register intercepts Windows actually uses.

Patch 28 (KVM: SVM: Keep TPR[3:0] across exits without a guest CR8
write) is a fix in its own right: the bug exists without planes, so
it should probably be applied standalone. I can send it separately
if wanted.

[RFC PATCH 10/13] KVM: planes: Let plane twins share XCR0
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


XCR0/XFEM is shared between VTLs (TLFS 15.4). The planes series keeps
arch.xcr0 per vCPU, so an XSETBV in one VTL is invisible in the other,
and VTL1 reads a stale XFEM: a feature VTL0 enabled (AVX, for the
Windows secure kernel) is not enabled in VTL1's XCR0. The register
contents themselves are a separate matter, carried by the code that
switches VTLs.

Add KVM_PLANE_SHARE_XCR0 to the KVM_CAP_PLANES share mask. With it set,
propagate the validated value to every plane twin in __kvm_set_xcr(),
covering both guest XSETBV and the KVM_SET_XCRS ioctl path, and let a
twin created later inherit plane 0's current value. The CPUID
dynamic-bits dirty marker lives in the shared common state, so the
existing single set covers the twins. Update the planes section of
api.rst and the kvm_cpuid_apply_runtime() comment, which still listed
XCR0 among the per-plane registers.

SEV-SNP VMPLs bank XCR0 per VMSA, and sharing it there would be wrong
in two ways: sev_es_sync_from_ghcb() feeds a VMPL's GHCB XCR0 to
__kvm_set_xcr(), so a less-privileged VMPL would overwrite its siblings'
values (and the MSR-protocol CPUID leaf 0xD sizes would then follow the
wrong VMPL); and a pre-launch KVM_SET_XCRS on one twin would change
plane 0's VMSA, and with it the launch measurement. An SNP VM cannot
set the share mask, since KVM_CAP_PLANES refuses its VM type, so its
VMPLs keep XCR0 of their own by construction.

The twins' fpstate sizing does not depend on this bit: the planes share
CPUID, so any twin's own XSETBV can enable a dynamic xfeature whether or
not XCR0 is shared.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
---
 Documentation/virt/kvm/api.rst  | 11 ++++++++---
 arch/x86/include/uapi/asm/kvm.h |  3 +++
 arch/x86/kvm/cpuid.c            |  4 +++-
 arch/x86/kvm/x86.c              | 34 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++

XCR0|DR6 on the system fd, 0 on a VM fd before the opt-in and the
enabled bits after it; an unknown bit and a non-zero args[2] fail with

through plane 0 before creating a plane-1 twin, and check that the twin

and check that plane 0 sees the guest's value only when DR6 is shared.

[RFC PATCH 00/13] KVM: planes: Userspace API
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


The VSM work¹ drives planes from userspace, which takes a little
more API than the planes series² itself provides: an explicit
userspace opt-in (enabling KVM_CAP_PLANES with the plane count,
before any vCPU is created), KVM_GET/SET_PLANE_RUN_STATE vCPU
ioctls, and the mechanics to go with them — per-plane requests
fanned out to the twin vCPUs, XCR0 shared across twins (with every
twin's fpstate sized for the dynamic xfeatures that allows), and
APICv inhibited while planes are enabled (for now). With selftests,
including Paolo's plane infrastructure tests from the original
planes RFC³, ported forward.

Posted separately from the VSM RFC v2 (to follow) so this part can
be reviewed — or folded — with the rest of the planes series. Based
on that series² with my fixes⁴ applied (including two follow-up
patches on that thread); the whole stack is at ⁵.

[RFC PATCH 05/13] KVM: selftests: Cover plane run-state ioctls in planes_test
Author: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>


Assert that a new plane-1 vCPU starts with KVM_PLANE_RUN_STATE_INACTIVE
and plane 0's with no flags, that every valid combination of
KVM_PLANE_RUN_STATE_USER_HOLD, KVM_PLANE_RUN_STATE_SUSPENDED and
KVM_PLANE_RUN_STATE_INACTIVE reads back as written (so a saved state
restores), and that clearing them reads back as runnable. INACTIVE on
plane 0, INACTIVE together with SUSPENDED and the lowest undefined bit
are refused with EINVAL, and a refused SET leaves an existing hold in
place. On a VM that did not enable more than one plane both ioctls fail
with EINVAL.

With plane 0 held and nothing runnable, KVM_RUN still honours
immediate_exit with EINTR, and otherwise exits with KVM_EXIT_PLANE_EVENT
and cause KVM_PLANE_EVENT_HELD, with kvm_run's if_flag and apic_base
filled in as on any other exit. That exit leaves registers marked in
kvm_dirty_regs unconsumed and unrefreshed, and once plane 1 is released
they land in plane 1, not plane 0. If plane 0 holds a pending I/O
completion, here from a real-mode OUT, a held KVM_RUN fails with EBUSY,
with or without immediate_exit, leaves the KVM_EXIT_IO in kvm_run and
plane 0's RIP on the OUT, and the OUT retires once plane 0 is released.

Signed-off-by: David Woodhouse <dwmw@amazon.co.uk>
